Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The two o'clock version

Fictional correction call using the lesson case.

  1. Technician: I need to correct my wording. The ten o'clock preliminary report wasn't final.
  2. Clinic nurse: We printed that version for the clinician. Is there now a different report?
  3. Technician: Yes, the final report was issued at two and supersedes the preliminary version for this request.
  4. Clinic nurse: Please give the issue time again so I can identify the correct document.
  5. Technician: Two o'clock is the final version. I can clarify the version, not interpret its findings.
  6. Clinic nurse: I have it open now. The heading and issue time both match your description.
  7. Technician: Could you alert the clinician that the earlier printout was preliminary and has been superseded?
  8. Clinic nurse: I will. Should I say the newer report proves the patient has no problem?
  9. Technician: No. That is a clinical conclusion the report's final status does not establish.
  10. Clinic nurse: Understood. I'll identify the current version and ask the clinician to interpret it with the patient.
A reference interval isn't the whole answer

Fictional patient-support call; no values or diagnoses are supplied.

  1. Clinic nurse: A caller asks whether being within the reference interval means every concern is ruled out.
  2. Technician: The interval is a comparison range for that result, not a conclusion about every clinical concern.
  3. Clinic nurse: Could you confirm the report's wording while leaving the patient's interpretation question with the clinician?
  4. Technician: Yes. I can identify the interval printed on the report and the version being discussed.
  5. Clinic nurse: The caller also compares it with a report from another laboratory using a different range.
  6. Technician: Different methods and reference populations may use different intervals; the clinician should review the actual reports.
  7. Clinic nurse: Please don't tell them one laboratory must be wrong simply because the ranges differ.
  8. Technician: I won't. A difference in printed ranges doesn't establish an error by itself.
  9. Clinic nurse: I'll arrange the clinician's explanation and make sure both reports are available for that discussion.
  10. Technician: That gives the clinician the sources and preserves the distinction between comparison information and clinical interpretation.
An amended report needs a notified recipient

Fictional laboratory has issued an amended report; receiving clinic has not acknowledged the notification.

  1. Technician: I'm contacting you about an amended report for request R22 issued this afternoon.
  2. Clinic coordinator: We already have a final report from yesterday. Is this just another copy of that one?
  3. Technician: No. Amended means an issued report has been revised through the laboratory's correction process.
  4. Clinic coordinator: Can you identify the version without discussing patient information on this unverified line?
  5. Technician: Yes. We'll use the established verified contact route for the report details and notification.
  6. Clinic coordinator: I can arrange that route now. Should I mark the clinician notified before we connect?
  7. Technician: Not yet. Arranging a connection doesn't establish that the responsible recipient received the amendment.
  8. Clinic coordinator: I'll connect the designated recipient and keep notification pending until the handoff is acknowledged.
  9. Technician: Thank you. The amendment's clinical meaning belongs with the clinician, but its changed version must be clear.
  10. Clinic coordinator: I'll identify the message as an amendment notification, not a routine duplicate report.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Course: Medical Laboratory Technician English
Professional audience: Medical laboratory technicians and clinical laboratory support staff
Scope: All cases and local procedures are fictional. This is English practice, not medical advice or laboratory operating instruction. Lesson 06: Preliminary, final, and amended reports
Original fictional case: The clinic received a preliminary report at 10:00 and a final report at 14:00 for the same request. Technician Noor mistakenly called the earlier version final. Clinic contact Priya asks which report status is correct. The 14:00 final report supersedes the 10:00 preliminary version for this request. No findings, clinical conclusions, or subsequent amended report are supplied. Noor needs to correct the earlier communication clearly and retain a traceable record.
Language workshop: Correcting the ten-o'clock report label
Communication goal: Withdraw an inaccurate report-status statement and identify the superseding final version without inventing changed findings or an amendment.
Language guidance: The 10:00 report was preliminary; calling it final was a communication error. The 14:00 final report supersedes that version. This sequence does not establish what any findings say or whether their content changed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I need to correct my earlier description. / I called the ten-o'clock report final. / That label was incorrect. / The ten-o'clock report was preliminary. / The fourteen-hundred report is final. / The final report supersedes the preliminary version for this request. / I am not saying the findings changed. / No later amended report is supplied here. / This is a correction to my communication. / Please confirm you have the corrected status. / Keep ten and fourteen attached to their respective versions. / Retain the earlier version according to the record policy. / Please use the final version's status in this enquiry. / No clinical conclusion is part of this correction. / Link the correction to the earlier message. / Ten was preliminary; fourteen is final and supersedes it.
Vocabulary: preliminary report: Report issued before the relevant final reporting stage. / final report: Report issued with final status under the applicable process. / amended report: Formally revised report issued through the applicable correction process. / supersede: Replace an earlier version as the applicable later record. / report version: Identified edition or stage of a report. / issue time: Recorded time a report was issued. / status label: Wording identifying a report's current stage. / communication correction: Explicit repair of an inaccurate spoken or written message. / detection limit: Lowest amount reliably detectable under the stated method conditions. / version control: Management of identifiable document revisions and their relationships. / current report: Report currently applicable under the stated reporting process. / superseded version: Earlier record replaced by an applicable later version. / correction trail: Record linking an error to its documented correction. / recipient acknowledgment: Confirmation that the intended recipient received the message. / report discrepancy: Difference in report information or status requiring clarification. / finding: Recorded result or observation in a report. / interpretation: Explanation of the meaning of a finding. / addendum: Additional report information issued under the applicable process. / revision reason: Documented explanation for a report change. / reissue: Issuance of a report again under the applicable procedure. / distribution record: Record of where a report or update was sent. / document history: Record of earlier versions and changes. / explicit correction: Statement directly identifying and fixing the prior error. / quantification limit: Lowest amount quantified with suitable precision and accuracy.
Speaking task: After checking the answers, read the explicit correction: ten hundred preliminary, fourteen hundred final. Switch roles and repeat both versions and times. Say communication correction without converting the sequence into an amended-report claim. Complete the four-line exchange. Keep the earlier mistake traceable and identify the current final version without inventing clinical findings.
Partner: Noor: Laboratory technician / Priya: Clinic contact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the report-status correction using only the supplied times and relationship.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noor: Can you confirm that you've received the corrected version information, rather than only seen that I sent a follow-up?
Priya: I confirm receipt: ten hundred preliminary, fourteen hundred final, and your earlier final label for ten hundred is withdrawn.
Noor: Thank you. No later amendment is established here. I'll use the same version wording in any onward communication.
Priya: I'll refer to the fourteen-hundred final version as the current report, without inventing findings or hiding the earlier correction.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Critical notifications and acknowledged handoffsSix point four, not six point one+

Your goal

Deliver and correct a critical-result message with an exact value, unit, complete read-back, and confirmed receipt.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Six point four, not six point one

Fictional Lumen laboratory designates C7's potassium result of 6.4 mmol/L as critical under its own criteria. The unit mmol/L means millimoles per liter. Case identity and Dr Shah's receiving authority are already verified. The report was issued at 14:10, and the call occurs at 14:12. Dr Shah is at extension 214 and initially repeats 6.1. Technician Noor must correct the value, obtain a complete read-back, and confirm receipt. No diagnosis, treatment, or universal critical threshold is supplied.

Noor: Laboratory technician / Dr Shah: Verified authorized recipient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What potassium value must be communicated?

Answer and explanation

C. 6.4 mmol/L

Lumen's supplied report value is 6.4 millimoles per liter, not the initial mistaken repeat.

2. What is already verified?

Answer and explanation

D. Case identity and Dr Shah's receiving authority

The briefing explicitly establishes identity and recipient authority before the call.

3. Which time is the call time?

Answer and explanation

D. 14:12

The report was issued at 14:10, while the notification call occurs at 14:12.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The correct value is 6.4 mmol/L, spoken six point four millimoles per liter. The incorrect 6.1 read-back must be corrected immediately and followed by a complete accurate repetition, not a vague yes or okay.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
critical result
Result requiring urgent notification under the laboratory's defined criteria.communicate a critical result
critical notification
Communication of a locally designated critical result.document the critical notification
potassium
Chemical element measured as an analyte in this fictional report.state the potassium result
millimoles per liter
Concentration unit written mmol/L.say millimoles per liter
numerical value
Number reported with the relevant analyte and unit.correct the numerical value
unit of measurement
Scale or quantity expression accompanying a value.retain the unit of measurement
decimal point
Mark separating the whole and fractional parts of a number.pronounce the decimal point
read-back
Recipient's spoken repetition of the received message.obtain a complete read-back
closed-loop communication
Exchange in which the recipient repeats and sender confirms the message.complete closed-loop communication
acknowledgment
Explicit confirmation that a message was received.obtain acknowledgment
authorized recipient
Person verified as permitted to receive the information.identify the authorized recipient
verified identity
Identity established through the required checks.preserve verified identity
report issue time
Time at which the report was issued.state the report issue time
notification time
Time at which the communication occurs.record the notification time
contact extension
Internal telephone number for the recipient.confirm the contact extension
discrepant repeat
Read-back differing from the intended message.correct a discrepant repeat
verbal correction
Spoken statement replacing inaccurate information.make a verbal correction
complete message
Required case reference, analyte, value, unit, and relevant status details.repeat the complete message
local critical criteria
Laboratory-specific rules defining critical designation.apply local critical criteria
notification record
Documentation of the communication and its receipt.complete the notification record
sender confirmation
Speaker's check that the repeated message is accurate.give sender confirmation
recipient role
Function or authority of the person receiving information.record the recipient role
clinical action
Patient-care decision made by an appropriately authorized professional.separate clinical action
communication discrepancy
Difference between intended and received information.resolve the communication discrepancy

Lumen alone supplies the critical designation for this fictional case. Report issue time is 14:10; notification time is 14:12; extension 214 is a contact number. None is a treatment instruction, diagnosis, or universal laboratory threshold.

C Notice the language

Interrupt an inaccurate repeat clearly

Politeness should not obscure a wrong number. Say correction, give the exact value and unit, and ask for the complete message again. This scenario begins after identity and receiving authority have been verified; the abbreviated case label does not replace real patient-identification requirements.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the notification
This is Lumen laboratory with a critical-result notification for verified case C7.
State the analyte
The analyte is potassium.
State the value and unit
The result is six point four millimoles per liter.
Attribute the designation
Lumen designates this result critical under its own criteria.
Give the report time
The report was issued at fourteen ten.
Request repetition
Please read back the case, analyte, value, and unit.
Interrupt the error
Correction: six point four, not six point one.
Repeat the whole value
Potassium, six point four millimoles per liter.
Request a complete second read-back
Please repeat the complete message again.
Confirm accuracy
That read-back is correct.
Confirm the recipient
You are Dr Shah at extension two one four.
Confirm receipt
Please confirm receipt of this critical-result notification.
Distinguish times
The report time is fourteen ten; this call is at fourteen twelve.
Keep the record exact
I will document the recipient, times, correction, and confirmed read-back.
Separate clinical decisions
This notification does not supply a diagnosis or treatment instruction.
Close the communication loop
The corrected value and unit have been read back accurately and receipt confirmed.
6 language and precision notes
Correction
Clearly interrupts an inaccurate number rather than politely allowing it to stand.
Not six point one
Identifies the exact mistaken value being replaced.
Complete
Requests analyte and unit as well as the number.
That ... is correct
Supplies the sender's confirmation after the recipient repeats.
Under its own criteria
Prevents the fictional critical designation becoming a universal threshold.
Report time versus call time
Keeps two distinct events from being combined in the record.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which correction is precise?

Answer and explanation

A. Correction: potassium 6.4 mmol/L, not 6.1; please read back the complete result

The correct response replaces the wrong value and requests complete confirmation.

2. Which read-back is complete for the result?

Answer and explanation

C. C7, potassium, 6.4 millimoles per liter

The complete result repeat includes the verified case reference, analyte, exact value, and unit.

3. What does 214 identify?

Answer and explanation

B. Dr Shah's contact extension

Extension 214 is the supplied recipient contact number, not a laboratory measurement.

4. Which conclusion is not authorized by the scenario?

Answer and explanation

B. A specific diagnosis or treatment plan

The scenario explicitly provides no diagnosis, treatment instruction, or universal threshold.

  4. Dr Shah
    C7, potassium, six point four 2, critical under Lumen's criteria, report issued at fourteen ten.
  5. Noor
    That read-back is correct. Please confirm receipt of the critical notification. Our case-identity and receiving-authority checks were completed before this exchange.

    I confirm 3 of the critical notification for C7: potassium six point four millimoles per liter, with the report issued at fourteen ten.
  7. Noor
    Thank you. This call is at fourteen twelve; the report issue time is fourteen ten. I'll keep those events separate.
  8. Dr Shah
    Agreed. Fourteen twelve is the 4, not a revised issue time.
  9. Noor
    Please confirm extension two-one-four for the communication record. The receiving identity and authority checks are already complete.
  10. Dr Shah
    Two-one-four is my 5. It isn't part of the concentration or the report time.
  11. Noor
    I'll record that the initial six-point-one repeat was corrected and the complete six-point-four message confirmed.
  12. Dr Shah
    Yes, retain that 6. The error was in my initial repetition, not a newly amended laboratory value.
  13. Noor
    Correct. I haven't changed the result. The report remains potassium six point four millimoles per liter.
  14. Dr Shah
    That is the confirmed 7. Keep the unit with it, not just the digits.
  15. Noor
    The laboratory notification is now acknowledged. No treatment instruction has been supplied in this call.

    Any 8 will go through the appropriate patient-care process. Please retain the notification record while I attend to that responsibility.

    I will retain C7, potassium, six point four, millimoles per liter, Lumen's critical designation, and fourteen-ten report time.
  18. Dr Shah
    That preserves the 9 I received, along with the separate call time and corrected read-back.
  19. Noor
    Your accurate repeat and acknowledgment are recorded. I won't substitute message sent for confirmed receipt.
  20. Dr Shah
    Thank you. That completes the 10 for the notification; the subsequent care decisions remain separate.

Answers and explanations
  1. read-back Turn 2. Read-back is the recipient's repetition, which initially contains the incorrect 6.1 value and must be corrected.
  2. millimoles per liter Turn 4. Millimoles per liter is the required concentration unit accompanying the corrected potassium value of 6.4.
  3. receipt Turn 6. Receipt confirms that the verified authorized recipient has received the corrected critical-result message.
  4. notification time Turn 8. Notification time is 14:12 for this call and must remain distinct from the report's 14:10 issue time.
  5. contact extension Turn 10. Contact extension 214 identifies Dr Shah's telephone contact and is not a result value or timestamp.
  6. communication discrepancy Turn 12. Communication discrepancy is the initial 6.1 repeat that was explicitly corrected to 6.4 and confirmed.
  7. numerical value Turn 14. Numerical value is 6.4, while the correction concerns the misheard repeat rather than a changed laboratory report.
  8. clinical action Turn 16. Clinical action belongs to the appropriate authorized patient-care process and is not supplied by this language-practice notification.
  9. complete message Turn 18. Complete message retains the case reference, potassium, corrected 6.4 value, unit, critical designation, and report time.
  10. closed-loop communication Turn 20. Closed-loop communication is completed through the corrected recipient repetition, sender confirmation, and explicit acknowledgment of receipt.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Noor: Dr Shah, this is Noor at Lumen laboratory. For verified case C7, potassium is six point four millimoles per liter, designated critical by Lumen. The report was issued at fourteen ten. Please read it back.
  2. Dr Shah: My read-back is C7, potassium, six point one millimoles per liter, report issued at fourteen ten. Have I received that correctly?
  3. Noor: Correction: six point four, not six point one. For C7, potassium is six point four millimoles per liter, critical under Lumen's criteria, report issued at fourteen ten. Please repeat the complete message.
  4. Dr Shah: C7, potassium, six point four millimoles per liter, critical under Lumen's criteria, report issued at fourteen ten.
  5. Noor: That read-back is correct. Please confirm receipt of the critical notification. Our case-identity and receiving-authority checks were completed before this exchange.
  6. Dr Shah: I confirm receipt of the critical notification for C7: potassium six point four millimoles per liter, with the report issued at fourteen ten.
  7. Noor: Thank you. This call is at fourteen twelve; the report issue time is fourteen ten. I'll keep those events separate.
  8. Dr Shah: Agreed. Fourteen twelve is the notification time, not a revised issue time.
  9. Noor: Please confirm extension two-one-four for the communication record. The receiving identity and authority checks are already complete.
  10. Dr Shah: Two-one-four is my contact extension. It isn't part of the concentration or the report time.
  11. Noor: I'll record that the initial six-point-one repeat was corrected and the complete six-point-four message confirmed.
  12. Dr Shah: Yes, retain that communication discrepancy. The error was in my initial repetition, not a newly amended laboratory value.
  13. Noor: Correct. I haven't changed the result. The report remains potassium six point four millimoles per liter.
  14. Dr Shah: That is the confirmed numerical value. Keep the unit with it, not just the digits.
  15. Noor: The laboratory notification is now acknowledged. No treatment instruction has been supplied in this call.
  16. Dr Shah: Any clinical action will go through the appropriate patient-care process. Please retain the notification record while I attend to that responsibility.
  17. Noor: I will retain C7, potassium, six point four, millimoles per liter, Lumen's critical designation, and fourteen-ten report time.
  18. Dr Shah: That preserves the complete message I received, along with the separate call time and corrected read-back.
  19. Noor: Your accurate repeat and acknowledgment are recorded. I won't substitute message sent for confirmed receipt.
  20. Dr Shah: Thank you. That completes the closed-loop communication for the notification; the subsequent care decisions remain separate.

Three more conversations at work

Six point four, not six point one

All facts fictional: Lumen designates C7 potassium 6.4 mmol/L critical under its own criteria. Identity and Dr Shah's receiving authority are already verified. Report issued 14:10, call at 14:12, contact extension 214. No universal threshold, diagnosis, or treatment is implied.

  1. Technician: Dr Shah, Noor from Lumen laboratory. For C7, the critical potassium result is six point four millimoles per liter.
  2. Dr Shah: C7, potassium, six point one millimoles per liter. Have I heard that correctly?
  3. Technician: It's six point four, not six point one. Please repeat the complete result.
  4. Dr Shah: C7, potassium, six point four millimoles per liter. Is that the value on the report?
  5. Technician: That matches, thank you. The report was issued at two ten this afternoon.
  6. Dr Shah: Is two ten the report's issue time, rather than the specimen collection time?
  7. Technician: Yes, two ten is the issue time. This call is taking place at two twelve.
  8. Dr Shah: Please record Dr Shah as the recipient. I've received C7's result, and my extension is 214.
  9. Technician: I'll record your acknowledgment at two twelve and extension two one four. Is that correct?
  10. Dr Shah: Yes, extension two one four. I have the potassium result and will review the report now.
The escalation contact accepts S9

Fictional Lumen case S9: sodium 121 mmol/L is designated critical by this lab. Report issued 11:06. First contact was unanswered at 11:07; immediate local escalation reached verified receiving nurse Elena at 11:08, extension 305. These are case facts, not a required waiting interval. No universal threshold or treatment is implied.

  1. Technician: Elena, Noor at Lumen laboratory. The first line was unanswered, so I'm calling about S9.
  2. Nurse Elena: Elena speaking. I'm covering S9 and can take the result. Please go ahead.
  3. Technician: The critical sodium result is one hundred twenty-one millimoles per liter, issued at eleven oh six.
  4. Nurse Elena: S9, sodium, one hundred twenty-one millimoles per liter. Please check that against the report.
  5. Technician: That read-back matches. Have you received the result and accepted the notification for S9?
  6. Nurse Elena: Yes, I have. Please use my name, Elena, and extension 305 for this contact.
  7. Technician: Elena, extension three zero five. This call is at eleven oh eight; the earlier attempt was unanswered.
  8. Nurse Elena: That's correct. Eleven oh eight is when I received it, not the earlier call time.
  9. Technician: I'll enter this acknowledgment separately in the notification log. The report is available under S9.
  10. Nurse Elena: Thank you. I have the result and units, and I'll bring the report to the care team.
The unit needs correcting too

Fictional Lumen case G4: glucose 48 mg/dL, meaning milligrams per deciliter, designated critical under this lab's criteria. Identity and receiving nurse Morgan's authority are verified. Report issued 13:40; Morgan's extension is 306. No universal threshold, diagnosis, or treatment is supplied.

  1. Technician: Morgan, Noor from Lumen laboratory. G4's critical glucose result is forty-eight milligrams per deciliter.
  2. Nurse Morgan: G4, glucose, forty-eight millimoles per liter. Is that the complete result you reported?
  3. Technician: The units are milligrams per deciliter, not millimoles per liter. The value is forty-eight.
  4. Nurse Morgan: I misheard the units. Please say the whole result once more so I can check it.
  5. Technician: For G4: glucose, forty-eight milligrams per deciliter. Please read that back to me.
  6. Nurse Morgan: G4, glucose, forty-eight milligrams per deciliter. That's the result I have recorded now.
  7. Technician: That read-back matches the report issued at one forty. Please confirm who is accepting it.
  8. Nurse Morgan: Morgan. I accept the notification, and you can reach me on extension 306.
  9. Technician: Morgan, extension three zero six. I'll record your acknowledgment and the corrected units; is that contact right?
  10. Nurse Morgan: Yes, three zero six. I have G4's glucose result in milligrams per deciliter.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Six point four, not six point one
  1. Check the answers, then read turns 1-6 in order. Correct six point one immediately to six point four millimoles per liter and confirm the complete repeat.
  2. Switch roles. Read the record details: report fourteen ten, notification fourteen twelve, extension two-one-four. Keep each number attached to its purpose.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct, repeat, confirm

Complete the result exchange. Identity and authority are already verified; the critical designation is specific to fictional Lumen.

Answer and explanation

C. 6.4

The supplied potassium value is 6.4, correcting the inaccurate initial repeat.

2. Recipient: "The unit means ___."

Answer and explanation

D. millimoles per liter

The abbreviation mmol/L expands to millimoles per liter.

3. Technician: "The report was issued at ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 14:10

Fourteen ten is the report issue time, distinct from the later call.

4. Recipient: "This notification call occurs at ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 14:12

Fourteen twelve is the supplied time of the critical-result notification call.

Language workshop: Six point four, not six point one
Communication goal: Deliver and correct a critical-result message with an exact value, unit, complete read-back, and confirmed receipt.
Language guidance: The correct value is 6.4 mmol/L, spoken six point four millimoles per liter. The incorrect 6.1 read-back must be corrected immediately and followed by a complete accurate repetition, not a vague yes or okay.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Referral laboratories and outstanding workDelivered parcel, unconfirmed accession, accepted handover+

Your goal

Distinguish courier delivery from referral acceptance and transfer a pending enquiry with explicit ownership and an update commitment.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Delivered parcel, unconfirmed accession, accepted handover

Send-out X8 appears on Monday's manifest. The courier confirms parcel delivery on Tuesday, but the referral laboratory has not confirmed accession acceptance for X8. Noor's shift is ending. Incoming technician Eli accepts responsibility for the enquiry and the agreed 15:00 clinic update. No result date is confirmed. The handover must preserve the difference between parcel delivery, individual accession acceptance, and the promise to provide an update rather than a result.

Noor: Outgoing laboratory technician / Eli: Incoming laboratory technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the courier confirm?

Answer and explanation

B. Parcel delivery on Tuesday

The courier confirms transport delivery, not the referral laboratory's individual accession decision.

2. What remains unconfirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. X8's accession acceptance

The referral laboratory has not confirmed accession acceptance for X8.

3. What does Eli accept?

Answer and explanation

D. The enquiry and the 15:00 clinic update

Eli explicitly accepts follow-up ownership and the agreed update, not a result deadline.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Monday's manifest records X8 in the outgoing shipment, and Tuesday's courier confirmation establishes parcel delivery. Neither document by itself establishes the referral laboratory's accession acceptance of X8.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
referral laboratory
External laboratory receiving work referred for testing.contact the referral laboratory
send-out
Specimen or request sent to another laboratory for work.track the send-out
manifest
Record listing items included in a shipment.check the manifest
courier
Service transporting the parcel.contact the courier
parcel delivery
Arrival of a transport package at its destination.confirm parcel delivery
accession acceptance
Referral laboratory's recorded acceptance of the individual request or specimen.confirm accession acceptance
delivery confirmation
Evidence that the transport package arrived.retain delivery confirmation
referral reference
Identifier used for the externally referred work.request the referral reference
tracking number
Transport identifier used to follow a shipment.verify the tracking number
chain of custody
Documented sequence of possession and transfer where required.preserve chain of custody
dispatch record
Record of sending an item onward.consult the dispatch record
outstanding enquiry
Follow-up question not yet resolved.transfer the outstanding enquiry
handover
Transfer of status, responsibility, and next actions.complete the handover
incoming technician
Staff member taking over work at a shift transition.identify the incoming technician
outgoing technician
Staff member transferring work before leaving.support the outgoing technician
action owner
Person explicitly responsible for the next task.name the action owner
acceptance acknowledgment
Explicit statement accepting a responsibility or received item.obtain acceptance acknowledgment
update time
Agreed time to communicate current status.preserve the update time
result date
Confirmed or proposed date for an issued finding.avoid inventing a result date
pending accession
Status where registration or acceptance remains unresolved.report pending accession
transport milestone
Recorded event in the movement of a parcel.distinguish a transport milestone
laboratory milestone
Recorded event in laboratory processing or acceptance.confirm the laboratory milestone
follow-up ownership
Assigned responsibility for continuing an enquiry.transfer follow-up ownership
continuity
Preservation of work and communication across a transition.maintain continuity

Eli accepts both the unresolved enquiry and the agreed 15:00 clinic update. That creates an explicit owner for follow-up, not a confirmed result date. The update may need to say acceptance is still unconfirmed if that remains the actual status.

C Notice the language

A message left is not a responsibility accepted

A useful handover ends with the incoming colleague naming the work they accept. Keep the exact unresolved question and the promised update time visible. Evidence of transport progress should not be upgraded to specimen acceptance or a confirmed result date.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the send-out
This handover concerns X8.
State the dispatch evidence
X8 appears on Monday's manifest.
State the transport evidence
The courier confirms parcel delivery on Tuesday.
Name the missing milestone
Referral accession acceptance is not confirmed.
Avoid upgrading evidence
Delivered does not mean individually accepted.
Name the enquiry
We need confirmation of X8's accession acceptance.
Transfer responsibility
Can you accept this outstanding enquiry?
Accept explicitly
I accept responsibility for following up X8.
Preserve the clinic commitment
The agreed clinic update is at fifteen hundred.
Separate result timing
No result date is confirmed.
Avoid inventing a reference
Do not create a referral accession number.
Keep the source visible
Attribute delivery to the courier and acceptance to the referral laboratory.
Request an accurate update
Report the actual status even if acceptance remains unresolved.
Preserve the record
Keep the manifest and delivery confirmation linked to X8.
Close the shift handover
Eli has accepted the enquiry and the clinic update.
Maintain the boundary
Follow-up accepted does not mean testing completed.
6 language and precision notes
Appears on
Attributes X8's dispatch listing to the manifest.
Confirms delivery
Limits the courier's evidence to transport.
Individually accepted
Distinguishes the specimen's laboratory status from its parcel.
I accept responsibility
Makes task ownership explicit.
Even if
Preserves the update obligation when the enquiry is still unresolved.
Does not mean
Prevents one completed milestone from implying another.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. X8 is on Monday's manifest; its parcel arrived Tuesday; accession acceptance is unconfirmed

The accurate summary keeps dispatch, delivery, and accession acceptance separate.

2. What is the precise follow-up question?

Answer and explanation

A. Has the referral laboratory accepted X8's accession?

The outstanding laboratory milestone is X8's individual accession acceptance.

3. What should happen at 15:00 under the agreed handover?

Answer and explanation

D. Eli gives the clinic the actual status

The commitment concerns a clinic update, not guaranteed testing completion.

4. Which statement confirms ownership?

Answer and explanation

A. I accept the X8 enquiry and the 15:00 clinic update

Eli's explicit acceptance transfers both the enquiry and the stated communication responsibility.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Delivered parcel, unconfirmed accession, accepted handover

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noor: Outgoing laboratory technician / Eli: Incoming laboratory technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • outstanding enquiry
  • update time
  • dispatch record
  • laboratory milestone
  • acceptance acknowledgment
  • referral reference
  • continuity
  • follow-up ownership
  • delivery confirmation
  • result date
  1. Noor
    Can you take over X8 before I leave? Monday's manifest lists it, and the parcel was delivered Tuesday, but one laboratory status is still missing.
  2. Eli
    What is the outstanding 1? I want to separate what the courier has confirmed from what the referral laboratory has actually acknowledged.
  3. Noor
    It's the individual accession acceptance. The referral laboratory hasn't confirmed accepting X8, even though the courier delivered the parcel.
  4. Eli
    Then the 2 is specifically about X8's accession acceptance. I will not treat parcel arrival as evidence that this particular item was accepted for work.
  5. Noor
    I'll leave you both records. The manifest supports dispatch; the courier record supports arrival. Neither is an issued-result notice.
  6. Eli
    I will preserve the 3 and the courier evidence. I will not replace those source details with a single vague status such as received and complete.
  7. Noor
    The clinic is expecting a status update at fifteen hundred. That commitment remains even if the acceptance enquiry is unresolved.
  8. Eli
    I accept the 4 of fifteen hundred as part of the handover. If accession acceptance remains unresolved, the update must state that accurately.
  9. Noor
    Can you explicitly take the enquiry as well as the call? I need to know who will continue it after this shift.
  10. Eli
    Yes. I accept 5 for X8's accession enquiry and the clinic update. I will carry both tasks forward under the laboratory's actual process.
  11. Noor
    The referral's accession field is blank in the information I have. X8 is our send-out reference, not a newly confirmed external accession.
  12. Eli
    Understood. A 6 needs the appropriate source. I will preserve X8 as the send-out reference while seeking the missing acceptance information.
  13. Noor
    If someone says received, please ask whether they mean the whole parcel or acceptance of the individual specimen.
  14. Eli
    I will call it 7, not accession confirmation. The referral laboratory must establish the individual laboratory status we are asking about.
  15. Noor
    We also have no result date. Please don't turn the fifteen-hundred update into a promise that the report will be ready.
  16. Eli
    No 8 is confirmed. I will not turn the fifteen-hundred update into a reporting promise or infer a date from the transport timeline.
  17. Noor
    I'll read back the handover: X8 dispatched Monday, parcel delivered Tuesday, accession acceptance outstanding; you own that enquiry and the fifteen-hundred update.
  18. Eli
    That is my 9 of the handover. I accept those two responsibilities, with the accession status and result timing still unresolved as stated.
  19. Noor
    Thanks. I'll record your acceptance before leaving. The unresolved enquiry now has an owner, but the laboratory status itself has not changed.
  20. Eli
    That maintains 10. I will follow up the acceptance question and give the clinic the actual status at fifteen hundred, without claiming a result that has not been confirmed.

Answers and explanations
  1. laboratory milestone Turn 2. Laboratory milestone is X8's individual accession acceptance, which is distinct from the confirmed transport delivery.
  2. outstanding enquiry Turn 4. Outstanding enquiry asks whether the referral laboratory has accepted X8, rather than whether its parcel reached the destination.
  3. dispatch record Turn 6. Dispatch record is the Monday manifest listing X8, which supports shipment history but not later acceptance or reporting.
  4. update time Turn 8. Update time is the agreed 15:00 clinic communication commitment, not a confirmed result deadline.
  5. follow-up ownership Turn 10. Follow-up ownership makes Eli responsible for continuing the X8 accession enquiry rather than merely receiving a note.
  6. referral reference Turn 12. Referral reference must come from the relevant confirmed record and must not be invented to make the handover appear complete.
  7. delivery confirmation Turn 14. Delivery confirmation establishes the parcel's arrival on Tuesday but not the referral laboratory's acceptance of every individual item.
  8. result date Turn 16. Result date remains unconfirmed and cannot be inferred from dispatch, parcel arrival, or acceptance of follow-up responsibility.
  9. acceptance acknowledgment Turn 18. Acceptance acknowledgment explicitly confirms Eli's responsibility for both the unresolved enquiry and the agreed clinic update.
  10. continuity Turn 20. Continuity preserves the unresolved work, evidence, and communication commitment across Noor's shift ending and Eli taking over.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Noor: Can you take over X8 before I leave? Monday's manifest lists it, and the parcel was delivered Tuesday, but one laboratory status is still missing.
  2. Eli: What is the outstanding laboratory milestone? I want to separate what the courier has confirmed from what the referral laboratory has actually acknowledged.
  3. Noor: It's the individual accession acceptance. The referral laboratory hasn't confirmed accepting X8, even though the courier delivered the parcel.
  4. Eli: Then the outstanding enquiry is specifically about X8's accession acceptance. I will not treat parcel arrival as evidence that this particular item was accepted for work.
  5. Noor: I'll leave you both records. The manifest supports dispatch; the courier record supports arrival. Neither is an issued-result notice.
  6. Eli: I will preserve the dispatch record and the courier evidence. I will not replace those source details with a single vague status such as received and complete.
  7. Noor: The clinic is expecting a status update at fifteen hundred. That commitment remains even if the acceptance enquiry is unresolved.
  8. Eli: I accept the update time of fifteen hundred as part of the handover. If accession acceptance remains unresolved, the update must state that accurately.
  9. Noor: Can you explicitly take the enquiry as well as the call? I need to know who will continue it after this shift.
  10. Eli: Yes. I accept follow-up ownership for X8's accession enquiry and the clinic update. I will carry both tasks forward under the laboratory's actual process.
  11. Noor: The referral's accession field is blank in the information I have. X8 is our send-out reference, not a newly confirmed external accession.
  12. Eli: Understood. A referral reference needs the appropriate source. I will preserve X8 as the send-out reference while seeking the missing acceptance information.
  13. Noor: If someone says received, please ask whether they mean the whole parcel or acceptance of the individual specimen.
  14. Eli: I will call it delivery confirmation, not accession confirmation. The referral laboratory must establish the individual laboratory status we are asking about.
  15. Noor: We also have no result date. Please don't turn the fifteen-hundred update into a promise that the report will be ready.
  16. Eli: No result date is confirmed. I will not turn the fifteen-hundred update into a reporting promise or infer a date from the transport timeline.
  17. Noor: I'll read back the handover: X8 dispatched Monday, parcel delivered Tuesday, accession acceptance outstanding; you own that enquiry and the fifteen-hundred update.
  18. Eli: That is my acceptance acknowledgment of the handover. I accept those two responsibilities, with the accession status and result timing still unresolved as stated.
  19. Noor: Thanks. I'll record your acceptance before leaving. The unresolved enquiry now has an owner, but the laboratory status itself has not changed.
  20. Eli: That maintains continuity. I will follow up the acceptance question and give the clinic the actual status at fifteen hundred, without claiming a result that has not been confirmed.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A delivered parcel with acceptance still open

Fictional X8 shift handoff using the lesson case.

  1. Outgoing technician: Send-out test X8 appears on Monday's shipping manifest. Tuesday's parcel delivery is confirmed, but accession acceptance isn't.
  2. Incoming technician: I'll take the enquiry. Does the clinic have a promised result time or only an update time?
  3. Outgoing technician: Only an update at three. There is no confirmed result date from the referral laboratory.
  4. Incoming technician: I accept that clinic update too. I'll ask the referral laboratory specifically about X8's acceptance.
  5. Outgoing technician: Please don't treat the courier signature as proof they registered every item in the parcel.
  6. Incoming technician: I won't. Parcel delivery and item-level acceptance remain separate in the handoff.
  7. Outgoing technician: The clinic also asked whether this means X8 is lost. We haven't established that.
  8. Incoming technician: I'll describe an acceptance enquiry, not a lost specimen, unless the receiving team confirms more.
  9. Outgoing technician: Then you own the external check and three o'clock update, with no promised report date.
  10. Incoming technician: Accepted. I'll record the response and keep unresolved items visible for the next shift.
A manifest entry without receipt confirmation

Fictional send-out coordinator compares a shipment list with the external receiving log.

  1. Technician: Our manifest lists S4 and S5, but your acknowledgment names only S4.
  2. Referral coordinator: I can confirm S4's registration. I need the receiving team to check S5 separately.
  3. Technician: Does the acknowledgment intentionally exclude S5, or is that reason still unknown?
  4. Referral coordinator: The reason is unknown. I shouldn't call S5 rejected simply because it isn't mentioned.
  5. Technician: Could you open an item-level enquiry using the manifest reference and the two separate identifiers?
  6. Referral coordinator: Yes. I'll ask the receiving team to compare the physical receipt information and registration records.
  7. Technician: Please keep S4's confirmed status unchanged while you investigate the separate S5 question.
  8. Referral coordinator: Agreed. I won't turn an unresolved item into an assumption about the entire shipment.
  9. Technician: Our clinic update will say S4 confirmed and S5 under receipt enquiry, without predicting a result date.
  10. Referral coordinator: That matches what I can establish now. I'll return the receiving team's finding when available.
Custody wording has a specific purpose

Fictional laboratory staff clarify documentation terminology without teaching collection or forensic procedures.

  1. Technician: The clinic asked for chain-of-custody documentation. Is that just another name for our routine courier receipt?
  2. Quality coordinator: No. Chain of custody is a specific documented record of possession and transfers where required.
  3. Technician: So I shouldn't rename a delivery signature as a complete chain-of-custody record.
  4. Quality coordinator: Correct. We need to establish the purpose and applicable requirements through the responsible laboratory team.
  5. Technician: Can you review what the clinic is requesting before we describe the documentation as sufficient?
  6. Quality coordinator: Yes. Send the enquiry through the designated record route, with the request purpose stated.
  7. Technician: They haven't told us the purpose yet. I'll obtain that clarification instead of assuming it.
  8. Quality coordinator: Good. Don't promise that a routine send-out meets requirements that haven't been identified.
  9. Technician: I'll explain that the courier receipt confirms its stated delivery event, not every possible documentation requirement.
  10. Quality coordinator: That is the useful distinction. I'll take the documentation review once the intended purpose is clarified.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Delivered parcel, unconfirmed accession, accepted handover

  2. Switch roles. Have Eli explicitly accept both the X8 enquiry and the fifteen-hundred clinic update, not just receipt of a note.

Transfer the question and the commitment

Complete the shift handover without upgrading parcel delivery to laboratory acceptance.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Outgoing: "The send-out reference is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. X8

X8 identifies the outstanding referral enquiry in the supplied handover.

2. Incoming: "The courier confirms delivery on ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Tuesday

Tuesday is the confirmed parcel-delivery day, not an accession-acceptance confirmation.

3. Outgoing: "Accession acceptance remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unconfirmed

The referral laboratory has not confirmed acceptance for the individual send-out.

4. Incoming: "I accept the enquiry and the ___ clinic update."

Answer and explanation

C. 15:00

Eli accepts responsibility for the agreed fifteen-hundred status update, not a result deadline.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 08: Referral laboratories and outstanding work
Original fictional case: Send-out X8 appears on Monday's manifest. The courier confirms parcel delivery on Tuesday, but the referral laboratory has not confirmed accession acceptance for X8. Noor's shift is ending. Incoming technician Eli accepts responsibility for the enquiry and the agreed 15:00 clinic update. No result date is confirmed. The handover must preserve the difference between parcel delivery, individual accession acceptance, and the promise to provide an update rather than a result.
Language workshop: Delivered parcel, unconfirmed accession, accepted handover
Communication goal: Distinguish courier delivery from referral acceptance and transfer a pending enquiry with explicit ownership and an update commitment.
Language guidance: Monday's manifest records X8 in the outgoing shipment, and Tuesday's courier confirmation establishes parcel delivery. Neither document by itself establishes the referral laboratory's accession acceptance of X8.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This handover concerns X8. / X8 appears on Monday's manifest. / The courier confirms parcel delivery on Tuesday. / Referral accession acceptance is not confirmed. / Delivered does not mean individually accepted. / We need confirmation of X8's accession acceptance. / Can you accept this outstanding enquiry? / I accept responsibility for following up X8. / The agreed clinic update is at fifteen hundred. / No result date is confirmed. / Do not create a referral accession number. / Attribute delivery to the courier and acceptance to the referral laboratory. / Report the actual status even if acceptance remains unresolved. / Keep the manifest and delivery confirmation linked to X8. / Eli has accepted the enquiry and the clinic update. / Follow-up accepted does not mean testing completed.
Vocabulary: referral laboratory: External laboratory receiving work referred for testing. / send-out: Specimen or request sent to another laboratory for work. / manifest: Record listing items included in a shipment. / courier: Service transporting the parcel. / parcel delivery: Arrival of a transport package at its destination. / accession acceptance: Referral laboratory's recorded acceptance of the individual request or specimen. / delivery confirmation: Evidence that the transport package arrived. / referral reference: Identifier used for the externally referred work. / tracking number: Transport identifier used to follow a shipment. / chain of custody: Documented sequence of possession and transfer where required. / dispatch record: Record of sending an item onward. / outstanding enquiry: Follow-up question not yet resolved. / handover: Transfer of status, responsibility, and next actions. / incoming technician: Staff member taking over work at a shift transition. / outgoing technician: Staff member transferring work before leaving. / action owner: Person explicitly responsible for the next task. / acceptance acknowledgment: Explicit statement accepting a responsibility or received item. / update time: Agreed time to communicate current status. / result date: Confirmed or proposed date for an issued finding. / pending accession: Status where registration or acceptance remains unresolved. / transport milestone: Recorded event in the movement of a parcel. / laboratory milestone: Recorded event in laboratory processing or acceptance. / follow-up ownership: Assigned responsibility for continuing an enquiry. / continuity: Preservation of work and communication across a transition.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue, then read the timeline: Monday dispatch, Tuesday parcel delivery, individual accession acceptance unconfirmed. Switch roles. Have Eli explicitly accept both the X8 enquiry and the fifteen-hundred clinic update, not just receipt of a note. Complete the four-line exchange. Preserve the absent result date and do not turn the update time into a reporting promise.
Partner: Noor: Outgoing laboratory technician / Eli: Incoming laboratory technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the shift handover without upgrading parcel delivery to laboratory acceptance.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noor: I'll read back the handover: X8 dispatched Monday, parcel delivered Tuesday, accession acceptance outstanding; you own that enquiry and the fifteen-hundred update.
Eli: That is my acceptance acknowledgment of the handover. I accept those two responsibilities, with the accession status and result timing still unresolved as stated.
Noor: Thanks. I'll record your acceptance before leaving. The unresolved enquiry now has an owner, but the laboratory status itself has not changed.
Eli: That maintains continuity. I will follow up the acceptance question and give the clinic the actual status at fifteen hundred, without claiming a result that has not been confirmed.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Three and a half standard deviations, not three and a half percent

Explain a control statistic with the correct denominator and a case-specific action rule.

Fictional control M: established mean 100 mg/L, standard deviation (SD) 2 mg/L, observed result 107 mg/L. The target statistics apply to this control and lot. Local rule: absolute z-score of 3 or more triggers a hold and authorized review. The affected reports are already withheld and the lead notified. Use z = (result - mean)/SD and coefficient of variation (CV) = SD/mean x 100%. No review outcome or restart permission is supplied.

Noor: Laboratory technician / Ben: Quality lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • review outcome
  • inclusive limit
  • standard deviation
  • z-score
  • coefficient of variation
  • trend
  1. Noor
    The worksheet says the control is three-point-five percent high. I think it mixes two different calculations. Can we correct that before the review?
  2. Ben
    Yes. The result is seven milligrams per liter above the established mean. Dividing that difference by the two-milligram-per-liter 1 gives three-point-five.
  3. Noor
    So the result is three-and-a-half standard deviations above the mean. For percentage difference, the denominator would be the mean of one hundred.
  4. Ben
    Exactly. The 2 is positive three-point-five, while the percentage above the mean is seven percent. Keep both labels with their numbers.
  5. Noor
    Our local hold rule includes an absolute score of exactly three. This result is above that boundary, and the hold is already in place.
  6. Ben
    Correct. An 3 includes equality. Don't rewrite the rule as greater than three when describing why it applies.
  7. Noor
    What does two divided by one hundred tell us? Someone has added two percent next to the control's historical statistics.
  8. Ben
    That is the 4 for the supplied statistics. It does not make this particular result acceptable or establish a completed review.
  9. Noor
    Would it be accurate to call this a trend? We have only this one observation in the exercise, not a sequence moving upward.
  10. Ben
    No. A 5 needs a pattern over time. A single point can trigger the stated rule without telling us why it occurred.
  11. Noor
    Then I should not write that calibration caused it, or that recalibration is the solution. Neither is in the evidence we have.
  12. Ben
    Right. The 6 remains pending. Preserve the result and its context rather than replacing an unexplained observation with a guessed cause.
  13. Noor
    On the Levey-Jennings display, I would locate this point above the plus-three-standard-deviation line, provided those are the same applicable target statistics.
  14. Ben
    Yes. The stated target and lot match here. A graph can make the distance visible, but the picture does not supply a missing investigation.
  15. Noor
    I'll revise the note to result one hundred seven, mean one hundred, SD two, all in milligrams per liter, and z positive three-point-five.
  16. Ben
    Include the separate seven-percent difference only if you label its denominator. Otherwise readers may mistake it for the CV or standardized score.
  17. Noor
    The established CV is two percent. I will not describe that as this run's newly demonstrated precision from one observation.
  18. Ben
    Good. Nor should the instrument's ready indicator be treated as cancellation of the report hold. Those are different kinds of status.
  19. Noor
    The handoff will preserve the held reports, notification already made, calculation correction, and pending authorized review.
  20. Ben
    That gives the next reviewer a usable record without turning a language exercise into instructions to adjust the analyzer or release results.

Answers and explanations
  1. standard deviation Turn 2. Standard deviation is the supplied spread statistic of 2 mg/L; dividing the 7 mg/L difference by it gives 3.5, not 3.5 percent.
  2. z-score Turn 4. The z-score is (107 - 100)/2 = +3.5, a unitless standardized distance above the established mean; the percentage difference is separately seven percent.
  3. inclusive limit Turn 6. The supplied local rule includes an absolute z-score equal to three, as well as values greater than three; it is not a universal laboratory rule.
  4. coefficient of variation Turn 8. The supplied established SD divided by its mean, multiplied by one hundred, is two percent; this CV describes those established statistics, not proof that the current run is acceptable.
  5. trend Turn 10. A trend requires a pattern across observations; the single supplied high point cannot establish a sustained directional pattern or its cause.
  6. review outcome Turn 12. No authorized review outcome is supplied; the hold and correct arithmetic do not establish a cause, technical remedy, or permission to release the affected reports.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Ben: Yes. The result is seven milligrams per liter above the established mean. Dividing that difference by the two-milligram-per-liter standard deviation gives three-point-five.

  6. Ben: Correct. An inclusive limit includes equality. Don't rewrite the rule as greater than three when describing why it applies.

Equality still triggers this local rule

New fictional control: established mean 200 mg/L, SD 5 mg/L, observed 215 mg/L. Same local rule: absolute z-score of 3 or more triggers a hold and review. The lead is notified; no release is authorized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: The result exceeds the mean by ___ mg/L.

Answer and explanation

D. 15

Subtract the established mean of two hundred from the observed result of two hundred fifteen.

2. Lead: The z-score is positive ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. three

The difference of fifteen divided by the SD of five equals positive three.

3. Technician: The established CV is ___ percent.

Answer and explanation

D. 2.5

Five divided by two hundred, multiplied by one hundred, equals two-point-five percent.

4. Lead: The affected reports remain ___ .

Answer and explanation

B. held

The case-specific rule includes equality at an absolute z-score of three, and no release is authorized.

The analyzer already applied the dilution factor

Reconcile raw and adjusted values without multiplying an already adjusted result twice.

Fictional training record: one sample volume plus four diluent volumes, factor 5. Raw diluted-sample reading: 40 mg/L. Confirmed analyzer flag: factor already applied; adjusted output: 200 mg/L. An unreleased laboratory information system (LIS) draft multiplies by 5 again, showing 1,000 mg/L. This is not a patient report or permission to perform a dilution, alter settings, or release results.

Noor: Laboratory technician / Imani: Laboratory systems specialist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reconciliation record
  • raw reading
  • factor-applied flag
  • dilution factor
  • double application
  • unit label
  1. Noor
    The instrument output is two hundred milligrams per liter, but the unreleased information-system draft says one thousand. I want to trace where the extra multiplication happened.
  2. Imani
    Start with the recorded 1. One sample volume plus four diluent volumes means a factor of five, not four.
  3. Noor
    The raw reading from the diluted material is forty milligrams per liter. Multiplying forty by five gives two hundred for the original-sample equivalent.
  4. Imani
    Correct. Keep the 2 separate from the adjusted output in the comparison. Both happen to use the same concentration unit.
  5. Noor
    The documented analyzer flag confirms that it already applied five. That is stronger evidence than guessing from the size of the displayed number.
  6. Imani
    Yes. The 3 explains what the output represents. Instrument and interface behavior must be checked, not assumed identical across systems.
  7. Noor
    Then the draft's second multiplication is the source of one thousand. It is five times the correctly adjusted output, not a different dilution.
  8. Imani
    That is 4 of the same factor. We need to preserve that distinction in the discrepancy record.
  9. Noor
    Could someone describe the difference as a units conversion? Forty, two hundred, and one thousand are all labeled milligrams per liter here.
  10. Imani
    No. The 5 has not changed. Don't attach a conversion explanation to an arithmetic step that applied a dilution factor again.
  11. Noor
    I can identify the mismatch, but that does not mean I should overwrite the draft or change the interface setting during this discussion.
  12. Imani
    Exactly. The 6 should retain the evidence and go through the authorized review and correction process.
  13. Noor
    I'll list raw forty, factor five, adjusted two hundred, draft one thousand, with the same unit alongside each concentration.
  14. Imani
    Also identify which field supplied each value. A screenshot of a number alone can lose the distinction between raw and adjusted fields.
  15. Noor
    We should keep the original discrepant draft traceable if an authorized correction is made. A clean final value would not explain what changed.
  16. Imani
    Agreed. Preserve the applicable change history. Do not remove the evidence needed to understand the repeated multiplication.
  17. Noor
    And we should not claim this proves every connected analyzer handles dilution the same way. This flag was verified for this record.
  18. Imani
    Right. The historical manufacturer example supports why system-specific handling matters, not a universal setting or current operating procedure.
  19. Noor
    My handoff will say the arithmetic is reconciled to two hundred, the draft differs by a repeated factor, and release remains unauthorized.
  20. Imani
    That separates a checked calculation from permission to issue a report. The responsible reviewer still needs to resolve the record through the actual laboratory process.

Answers and explanations
  1. dilution factor Turn 2. One volume of sample plus four volumes of diluent gives five final volumes per one sample volume, so the recorded dilution factor is five, not four.
  2. raw reading Turn 4. The raw diluted-sample reading is forty mg/L before the factor is applied; it must remain distinguishable from the adjusted two-hundred-mg/L output.
  3. factor-applied flag Turn 6. The setup explicitly confirms the analyzer's documented flag, so its output of two hundred mg/L already includes the factor and must not be treated as an unadjusted reading.
  4. double application Turn 8. Multiplying the already adjusted two hundred by five produces one thousand, applying the same dilution factor twice rather than adding new measurement information.
  5. unit label Turn 10. All three supplied values use mg/L, so the discrepancy is not an mg/L-to-another-unit conversion; the extra multiplication is the documented difference.
  6. reconciliation record Turn 12. The reconciliation record should retain the raw value, factor, confirmed flag, adjusted output and discrepant draft for authorized review; identifying the mismatch does not itself authorize edits or release.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Noor: The instrument output is two hundred milligrams per liter, but the unreleased information-system draft says one thousand. I want to trace where the extra multiplication happened.
  2. Imani: Start with the recorded dilution factor. One sample volume plus four diluent volumes means a factor of five, not four.
  3. Noor: The raw reading from the diluted material is forty milligrams per liter. Multiplying forty by five gives two hundred for the original-sample equivalent.
  4. Imani: Correct. Keep the raw reading separate from the adjusted output in the comparison. Both happen to use the same concentration unit.
  5. Noor: The documented analyzer flag confirms that it already applied five. That is stronger evidence than guessing from the size of the displayed number.
  6. Imani: Yes. The factor-applied flag explains what the output represents. Instrument and interface behavior must be checked, not assumed identical across systems.
  7. Noor: Then the draft's second multiplication is the source of one thousand. It is five times the correctly adjusted output, not a different dilution.
  8. Imani: That is double application of the same factor. We need to preserve that distinction in the discrepancy record.
  9. Noor: Could someone describe the difference as a units conversion? Forty, two hundred, and one thousand are all labeled milligrams per liter here.
  10. Imani: No. The unit label has not changed. Don't attach a conversion explanation to an arithmetic step that applied a dilution factor again.
  11. Noor: I can identify the mismatch, but that does not mean I should overwrite the draft or change the interface setting during this discussion.
  12. Imani: Exactly. The reconciliation record should retain the evidence and go through the authorized review and correction process.
  13. Noor: I'll list raw forty, factor five, adjusted two hundred, draft one thousand, with the same unit alongside each concentration.

Identify the second multiplication

New fictional record: one sample volume plus nine diluent volumes; raw reading 7 mg/L. The confirmed analyzer flag says the factor was already applied, and output is 70 mg/L. An unreleased draft shows 700 mg/L after multiplying again. Authorized review is not complete.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

Answer and explanation

B. 10

One sample volume plus nine diluent volumes gives ten final volumes per sample volume.

2. Specialist: The once-adjusted concentration is ___ mg/L.

Answer and explanation

B. 70

Seven multiplied by the factor of ten equals seventy, which is the supplied adjusted analyzer output.

3. Technician: The twice-adjusted draft incorrectly shows ___ mg/L.

Answer and explanation

C. 700

4. Specialist: Authorized review is still ___ .

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The calculation can be checked, but the setup states that authorized review has not been completed.
